GR_SALL_Transcript.md
# Series: Systematically Attacking The Legs
# Instructor: Gordon Ryan
# Volumes: 1–8
# Context: In this comprehensive series, Gordon Ryan breaks down the modern leg lock game in detail, covering the philosophy, positional hierarchies, control mechanics, and finishing techniques that revolutionized no-gi grappling. Across eight volumes, he explains how to create asymmetry in leg entanglements, control transitions between Ashi Garami variations, and apply high-percentage submissions including inside and outside heel hooks, toe holds, knee bars, and Achilles locks. Ryan also explores strategic dilemmas, defensive counters, and live rolling applications, making this one of the most complete resources on leg attacks ever produced.

she's going to be getting broken sooner from the toe hold and then she'll have to go into a defensive role and then i'll switch my position into whatever i want as far as attacks go okay so if you're hooks will but they ultimately won't be mechanically as strong as heel hooks because of the fact that your hands are applying the lock rather than having your partner's leg connected to your full body where now your body is doing the breaking okay so the toe hold pressure comes on first but it's not mechanically as strong as a heel hook okay so just going into mechanics and a little bit of strategy holds come on faster but they're not mechanically as strong as heel hooks okay when I'm breaking with a toe hold I'm looking for a shearing force a coupled force two forces in two directions like John always references if I ask you to rip a piece of paper with one hand you wouldn't be able to do it you need one force in one direction and an opposing force in the opposite direction to actually tear the paper same is true with toe holds if I just push my partner's toes to her butt you see this in competition all the time people lock up toe holds they're cranking on the toe hold the toes go into (52:50 - 56:30)
So that was a good transition there, so you see there, he goes to backside 50-50, where he gets his knee to the floor, he goes to expose my heel, I recognise, I rotate, I have my knee in the middle now, and then I pass to a cross Ashi, let's take a look at that, so he goes, I have my knee in the middle, I use that knee in the middle to split his legs, so I go to a post with my foot on the far hamstring to a stretch Ashi, see my foot in there, and I fire my right leg over into cross Ashi, and I pass it across to an inside Ashi, I expose the heel, I roll him through switch to an outside Ashi, and then we get to a finish. All right, so these are the major Ashi Garamis that we use all the time, Rimi Ashi Garami, which is the most common one, the easiest one to enter, just entering into Ashi Garami, and outside Ashi Garami, the staple of breaking someone's leg from outside heel up positions, usually start in a Rimi roll to outside, so that's very important. Mutual Ashi Garami, which is a situation where you both have outside heel ups, and that just gets you into the idea of being able to pursue your own attacks while also hiding your legs from the threats of your opponent's leg attacks.
Cross Ashi, which is the most dominant leg locking position you can be in, where you have a cross Ashi Garami, where your legs are safe, and you have mechanically the strongest breaking position you can be in as far as leg locks go, where you have inverted heel looks, and you're safe from their attacks. And then 50-50, where you're basically just banking on the fact that you're better than the other guy, and you both have the equal amount of attacks based on whoever has more knowledge. It's 50-50, you're in a symmetrical position versus an asymmetrical situation, so you have a very strong break from there, but you're basically just banking on the fact that you're better than the other person at that position.
So they all are used in a different array of situations. There's times for 50-50, there's times for outside Ashi, times for Rimi Ashi Garami, and you as the viewer, your job again is to be able to recognise that and use the certain specific moves in the correct time frame when you're in each certain situation. There's always times for certain moves, and there's times that you shouldn't be using moves, so your job as the student is to recognise when to use certain moves and recognise when not to.
All right, so hopefully you guys enjoyed this. I've had a lot of positive reviews about the Roland commentary, seeing everything I'm teaching being used live, so hopefully you enjoyed another one of these, and hopefully you start breaking some legs.
